## Features

### Viewing

Viewing is basically free and possible without logging in (in Provisional, β, and since February 28, 2018 [^475]). Registration is required for posting comments and similar activities.

#### Economy mode

During busy periods—weekdays 18:00–26:00, and Saturdays, Sundays, and public holidays 12:00–26:00—free ordinary members and logged-out viewers received low-bitrate video (Economy Mode) to conserve niconico bandwidth [^476].[^tn-hours]

Economy Mode played server-side re-encodes with lower image and audio quality. Depending on the video, this could occasionally cause audio synchronization problems, shorten playback time, or, more rarely, produce mosaic-like picture corruption.

Economy Mode originally began at 19:00. In February 2009, the period expanded to start at 18:00 on weekdays and noon on holidays [^477]. On January 23, 2010, the holiday start moved earlier again, to 10:00.

It was abolished in the major service overhaul of March 1, 2024. The maximum selectable quality for ordinary members and logged-out users was also relaxed to 720p.

### Comment system

![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Japanese_Danmaku_example.jpg/500px-Japanese_Danmaku_example.jpg)

An illustration of *danmaku* comments.

Viewers can write comments over the playback screen. A comment is posted at the current point in the video's timeline; on later playbacks, it appears at that point and traverses the screen from right to left over three seconds. Commands described below can also position comments like subtitles at the top or bottom. This distinctive system is Niconico Video's defining feature, for which Dwango holds a patent [^478]. In 2023, a patent-infringement lawsuit was heard in the Intellectual Property High Court, with Dwango winning and FC2 losing [^479]. In 2025, the Supreme Court also recognized infringement, with the same outcome: it ordered FC2 Video to stop distribution and pay over ¥110 million in damages [^480] [^481] [^482].

Comment timing is entirely based on the video's timeline, independently of the actual clock time of posting. Consequently, even comments posted at different times appear at the same video moment at which they were entered, with one's own comments coexisting with earlier and later posts. Unlike the chronological organization of chat and bulletin boards, this allows viewers to transcend real time and share a simulated moment; the operators call it “asynchronous live.” Media may be classified as synchronous or asynchronous according to whether there is a gap between transmission and reception [^4]. Niconico comments have sometimes been characterized as **pseudo-synchronous**: posting and reading actually occur at different real-world times, but coincide on the playback timeline and thus feel synchronous [^483] [^484]. By introducing time into commenting, Niconico achieved a sense of unity between users absent from conventional video-upload services. Some users, called **craftspeople** (*shokunin*), decorate videos with comment-based ASCII art (AA), or add lyrics to *utattemita* (“tried singing”) videos. At especially exciting moments, simultaneous comments may cover the entire screen; these are called **danmaku**, or “barrages” [^485].[^tn-temita]

Because comments overlay the picture, viewers who find them obstructive can select Hide Comments. The default is to display them, and the hidden setting is not remembered; the player warns that it is “forgetful.”

Once comments exceed a specified count, older comments stop appearing. Registering a video in My Memory preserves the comments at that moment. Individual comments can also be shown or hidden when viewing a saved My Memory.

Through γ, commenters could attach their names. Most posted casually, and very few used a handle, so the name field was abolished when RC launched.

#### Commands

Normal comments are white text scrolling right to left. Commands offer several colors, sizes, and display methods, allowing flexible placement. They are entered in the box left of the comment field, for example `big`, `red`, or `ue`; different attributes, such as position and color, can be combined. Ordinary members have eight colors; paid Premium members can use all colors through HTML color codes.

This can be used to add subtitles through comments. Complex combinations of commands and special Unicode characters can form illustrations and enormous letters, known as “comment art” (CA), after ASCII art (AA). CA sometimes incorporates animation.

#### Uploader comments

Uploaders can add special comments to their own videos [^486]. Distinguished from ordinary comments, these do not disappear when the display limit is exceeded, and their display duration can be set. Initially only Premium members and the uploader could view videos using uploader comments; ordinary members can now view them too.

#### Filters

Uploaders can replace particular words entered in comments on their videos with other specified words: for example, “Tanaka” → “Satō,” or `u` → `ｳｯ-ｳｯ-ｳﾏｳﾏ` (“U-u-umauma”).

#### Nicoscript

Nicoscript is available to uploaders and users [^487]. It enables activities using comments, such as polls, or cutting a hole in part of a video and moving it with comments.

#### Blocking settings

These hide comments containing specified words or posted by specified users. Users can configure words, encrypted user IDs, and commands, up to a combined 20 entries, or 100 for Premium members. Unlike hiding all comments, this hides only comments the viewer finds objectionable.

#### Number of displayed comments

All comments posted since the service began are stored [^5]; video deletion, elapsed time, or their number do not erase them. However, the number displayed simultaneously is limited according to video length, with older comments disappearing first when exceeded. Premium members can specify a date and time through Past Logs to view older comments, subject to the same display limit.

The maximum number has changed several times:

- Initially, every posted comment was displayed.
- **December 19, 2006:** display was limited to the latest 250 comments.
- **January 5, 2007:** limits varied by video length: 250 for videos under five minutes, 500 for five to under ten minutes, and 1,000 for ten minutes or longer.
- **February 1, 2008, 15:00:** videos under two and a half minutes were limited to the latest 100. Many users called this too strict, and it was relaxed three days later.
- **February 4, 2008, 17:00:** the 100-comment limit was restricted to videos under one minute; videos from one to under two and a half minutes returned to 250.
- **December 22, 2010:** the limit became “the latest 100 per minute” [^488]. Instead of a whole-video limit, the timeline was divided into one-minute segments and the latest 100 comments retrieved for each [^489]. This prevented clustering at particular moments and maintained a consistent comment density for awkward lengths such as four or nine minutes, and for videos much longer than ten minutes. The old retrieval behavior remained selectable through Reduce Comments in the player menu.
- **February 3, 2011:** comments from both the new “latest 100 per minute” and the old “latest 100–1,000 across the whole video” systems were combined [^490]. A video exactly ten minutes long could thus display up to 1,900 comments, depending on circumstances; comments lost under the new method became visible again as before the change.

#### Comment tendencies

Posted comments frequently contain Internet slang [^491].

#### Abuse exploiting vulnerabilities

From around early April 2007 [^492], attacks using unauthorized tools were discovered artificially increasing views and comments to manipulate rankings [^493]. Some affected videos had their view and comment counts reset [^492].

Around the same time, upload URLs were exploited to produce abnormal numbers of duplicate uploads. The operators warned that such misconduct could lead to account deletion [^494].

#### Niconico Activity Guidelines: comment deletion

Users can report malicious behavior through Report Inappropriate Comments below the playback page, opening a deletion-request board [^495] using the same floating-thread format as [[2channel Hub|2channel]], and request removal of problematic comments [^496]. Generally, a staff member with the handle “Hissori Sakujonin” (“Quiet Deleter”) and comment-removal authority is present; when deletion is warranted, action is taken shortly after the report with the standard reply “Handled.” The board also accepts reports of misconduct outside the comment feature, and operators may suspend commenting temporarily or for longer periods. Reports can address harassment such as “die,” and inappropriate tags, as well as trolling. Because reports are handled on a bulletin board, their contents are visible to all users [^495].

Uploaders also have Comment Editing authority and can hide individual comments, effectively deleting them [^497]. Unlike operator deletion, this merely sets a hidden flag in the data and hides the comment in the player. It can therefore be reversed, and the text remains readable in raw server data.

As growing user numbers nevertheless led to increasingly hostile comment sections, the operators announced the Niconico Activity Guidelines on October 1, 2015 in a notice titled “To Our Users” [^498]. The guidelines stated that comments could also be removed for harassment or whenever operators judged them problematic for service operation.

### Nicoru

#### Overview

Nicoru is a feature pressed like a “Like” for a favorite video, comment, or other item. Introduced in Niconico Video:ZERO, it was abolished June 18, 2015, then revived in a revamped form August 29, 2019. It was popular with some users; after its abolition, software called Pseudo-Nicoru was even made to reproduce it.[^tn-nicoru]

#### Development

##### Original Nicoru

- April 27, 2012 — Nicoru was announced at an advance press presentation of new Niconico Video services [^499].
- May 1, 2012 — The Nicoru button launched alongside Niconico Video:Zero and Niconico Live:Zero, initially for Premium members.
- May 28, 2015 — The operators announced its closure around 14:00 June 18, including new likes, color changes, and total likes received on My Page [^500].
- June 18, 2015 — Service ended around 14:00. Histories of likes given and received remained viewable on My Page.
- October 8, 2015 — A user recreated some features as a Google Chrome plugin named Similar Nicoru.
- April 19, 2017 — On [[Dwango|Dwango's]] newly launched Mastodon instance friends.nico, Favorites was renamed Nicoru in response to user requests [^501].

##### New Nicoru

- April 28, 2018 — At Niconico Chōkaigi 2018, operations head Shigetaka Kurita announced on Twitter that Nicoru would return with new specifications [^502]. Earlier histories of likes given and received would cease to be viewable.
- March 11, 2019 — An operations live broadcast announced Nicoru's return on PC Niconico Video [^503].
- August 29, 2019 — The alpha version launched exclusively for Premium members using the PC HTML5 player [^504].
- October 3, 2019 — The beta added direct liking of scrolling comments and undoing likes, plus like counts in the comment list. After consideration, a feature emphasizing liked comments on the video itself was abandoned [^505].
- September 1, 2020 — A comment liked by the uploader gained an uploader-like indicator and remained displayed longer [^506].
- June 4, 2022 — Asked on Twitter about opening Nicoru to ordinary members, current director Shigetaka Kurita replied: “If we open Nicoru to everyone, it could become a target for manipulation, so there are currently no plans.”
- November 21, 2024 — The final version launched, with notifications of comments being liked and plans to open selected functions to ordinary members.

#### Differences between original and new Nicoru

The original Nicoru, offered from 2012 to 2015, and the new Nicoru, offered since 2019, differ in functionality.

| Item | Original Nicoru | New Nicoru |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Eligible services | Videos, comments, tags, Niconico Market products, Nicorepo, video reviews | Video comments only |
| Where likes can be given | Comment panel, video player, comment list | Comment panel; player and comment-list support planned for future updates |
| Liked comments | Text color changes | Background color changes |
| Repeated likes on the same comment | Possible | Impossible |
| Frequently liked comments | No special feature | More likely to remain on the video. Claims that this also occurred in the original were an urban legend/rumor; it was formally adopted in the new version. |
| Identify likers from My Page | Possible | Impossible |
| Public histories of likes received/given | Choice of public/private; compulsorily public, respectively | Private |
| Notifications of likes received | Yes | Yes |

### Search

Videos can be searched by keywords in their titles and descriptions, and by tags. Results can be sorted ascending or descending by upload time, views, most recent comment, and other fields. Through the Summer version, Random Search also selected arbitrary videos registered on the site.

### Tags and categories

Besides its description, each video can have up to ten **tags**, search keywords indicating its content. Tags make similar videos easy to find. Since May 2008, integration with Niconico Pedia has provided explanations and histories of tags and representative tagged videos.

Distinctively, viewers as well as uploaders can add tags freely. Though intended for searching, tags also comment on video content and express Niconico-specific humor, including “a waste of talent” and “Kōmei's trap.” In practice, they may highlight a video's attractions rather than merely classify it [^507], or become conversations between users (“conversational tags”) [^508]. Viewers spontaneously invent tags for videos sharing material—among the countless subgenres of popular categories such as Tried Singing or The Idolmaster—or sharing an uploader, supporting more specific searches [^509] [^510].[^tn-tags]

However, this system can produce meta-noise that obstructs searches and enable harassment through tags. Prominent and highly ranked videos may experience “tag wars,” users repeatedly adding and deleting tags they consider appropriate, resembling edit wars on the online encyclopedia Wikipedia [^511] (online encyclopedia). Videos may deliberately lack tags to avoid trolling or prevent removal for copyright or potential terms violations. Information engineer Itō (伊藤聖修) argues that the finite ten-tag limit creates selection through tag wars over time, leaving better tags [^512]. Satoshi Hamano notes that intensified tag wars can spread a tag to other videos and thereby expand possible interpretations of the newly tagged works [^508].

To counter tag-deletion abuse, uploaders can lock up to five tags against viewer removal.

Certain words—Games, Entertainment, Cooking, Music, and others—are category tags and function as video categories. Only the uploader can set the main category by locking its tag, making the video eligible for category rankings. Originally only one main category could be registered. When categories such as The Idolmaster, Touhou, and VOCALOID were added, established combinations such as The Idolmaster/Games or VOCALOID/Music became impossible. Up to two subsidiary category tags were therefore allowed for search purposes, making three category tags in total.

Tags also direct users to other videos, for example “Continued → sm123456.” Clicking a tag containing a video ID displays a link to that video as a “Did you mean?” suggestion on the search page.

Each language version has separate tags; from the Japanese version's perspective, other versions' tags are “overseas tags.” They were initially viewable after other-language sites appeared, but can no longer be viewed from the Japanese version, except on Niconico Pedia video-article pages; access between other language versions remains possible. Other-language tags can only be edited by visiting that language's site.

#### Niconico Pedia

An online encyclopedia linked from Niconico Video tags.

For details, see Niconico Pedia.

### Mylists

Mylists collect multiple favorite videos into lists, which can be made public. Besides personal bookmarking, uploaders commonly publish lists of their own work. They support RSS. Features also include searching public Mylists containing the current video and email notifications of updates to a particular list; email notifications ended October 1, 2009.

Mylist registration counts are harder to manipulate than views or comments and more directly reflect appreciation: an unpopular video may be watched or commented on without being saved. They therefore serve as popularity indicators in rankings described below. Continuous Mylist playback was experimentally implemented September 8, 2010, and shuffle playback September 14, with formal implementation planned within September.

### Niconi Advertising

Niconi Advertising lets users spend Niconico Points to promote favorite videos or their own uploads. Advertisers' names appear at the end of promoted videos in the style of television sponsorship credits. See Niconi Advertising for details.

### Uploading videos

Videos can be uploaded through PC niconico and smartphone-browser niconico [^513].

Through β, Niconico Video had no upload function and only supported videos sourced from YouTube, AmebaVision, and Photozou. Most videos effectively came from YouTube. YouTube's complete blocking of Niconico access forced β to close; YouTube support was dropped entirely when γ launched. SMILEVIDEO was prepared as its replacement. YouTube did not officially explain the block, but Niwango inferred that its “free-riding” service, directly referencing YouTube videos, had grown explosively in traffic and placed a heavy load on YouTube servers [^514].

AmebaVision uploads remained supported until partway through RC, ending at maintenance on October 1, 2007 for operational reasons. CyberAgent explained that users suspended from SMILEVIDEO for illegal, obscene, or otherwise prohibited uploads increasingly obtained AmebaVision accounts to upload the same problematic videos again [^515]. Photozou videos were also supported, but new submissions ended at maintenance on May 28, 2008.

From the opening of β until October 29, 2010, uploading and uploaded-video management alone were separated onto [[SMILEVIDEO]] at smilevideo.jp. This dedicated upload site had no viewing function; all uploads were automatically registered on Niconico Video. All formerly separate SMILEVIDEO features and uploaded videos are now integrated into Niconico My Page.

As videos and users increased, operating costs rose; Flash's decline and increasingly diverse user environments also prompted a redesign of the video-delivery infrastructure in 2015–2016. The new Dwango Media Cluster (DMC) began handling some users' uploads in August 2016 and covered all users' uploads by December 2016 [^516] [^517].

Every uploaded video is re-encoded on the server, so uploading the original file yields the highest quality.

#### Replacing uploaded videos

Premium members can replace a video with a corrected upload within 24 hours of publication. Existing comments and view counts carry over, and a separate indicator identifies the video as corrected.

After one replacement, another requires a one-hour interval.

### Badges

Badges are proofs of achievement awarded for meeting particular conditions on Niconico [^518].

#### Overview

Earned badges can be viewed on My Page. They replaced the similar Stamps feature; new Stamps ceased to be issued March 12, 2019 [^519], although existing ones remained viewable on My Page.

##### Improvements

- Stronger discovery-related features: users can view the video or user responsible for an award, making their role in discovering content visible.
- Continuous Premium now uses cumulative membership duration.

#### History

The history of Badges is as follows [^520].

##### 2018

- August 13 — Award conditions became explicit, and the required achievement counts for higher grades changed.
- December 19 — Required counts for higher grades of the Break Veteran badge changed.

##### 2019

- January 9 — The Contribution Equation Pioneer badge and Hidden Badges were added, along with progress meters toward the next grade. Users could access badge details from another user's badge list.
- March 12 — New Stamps ceased to be issued.
- March 28 — The Break feature ended, so new Break Veteran badges ceased to be issued.
- April 11 — Instigator conditions changed. Experimental Broadcasts ended, so new Breaking the Silence badges ceased to be issued.
- April 23 — Instigator grades 4–10 were unlocked.
- July 9 — The Live Game Ranker badge was added. Broadcasters earning it received an award animation, and the badges held by the top five users were displayed when live-game rankings were announced.
- October 23 — The Autumn Live Game Quest ran through October 29 [^521]. Winning a ranked game earned its badge.
- October 31 — Live Game Quest Master badges were distributed to the top 100 users at the end of the Autumn Live Game Quest.
- December 23 — New Year Tsurikkuma ran through January 6, 2020 [^522]. Finishing in the top five in Tsurikkuma earned its badge.

##### 2020

- January 8 — Tsurikkumaster badges were distributed to the top 100 users at the end of New Year Tsurikkuma.
- February 26 — Live Game Ranker became unobtainable in community-only or channel-only broadcasts.
- April 14 — The Spring Editing Festival ran through April 20 [^523]. Creating or editing Niconico Pedia articles, or posting Oekakiko drawings or Pikokakiko music, earned the “Pedia! Spring Editing Festival” badge.
- April 22 — “Pedia! Quest Master” badges were distributed to the top 100 users at the end of the Spring Editing Festival.

### Stamps (discontinued)

Stamps were a service formerly offered by [[Dwango]], awarded for satisfying conditions through use of Niconico services [^524].

#### Overview

Each stamp carried experience points according to rarity; rarer stamps awarded more points [^525]. These determined ranking positions. Once acquired, stamps could not be deleted [^526].

Badges, a revamped version of Stamps, launched in 2018 [^527]. Stamp issuance ended March 12, 2019 [^528], but earlier stamps remained viewable on My Page.

#### History

**2010**

- October 29 — Stamps launched alongside Niconico Video (Harajuku) [^529].

**2011**

- February 17 — The stamp album began displaying the proportion of stamps acquired [^530].
- September 15 — Niconico Apps stamps were released [^531].
- December 22 — The “Tried Visiting Niconico Headquarters” stamp was added, obtainable by holding a mobile phone over the Niconico Machine at Niconico Headquarters [^532].

**2012**

- August 27 — Distribution of the Nicowari Survey stamp was completed for participants in the Million-Person Survey held August 5–8 [^533].

**2019**

- March 12 — Stamp issuance ended.

## Niconico services

### Niconi Commons

Professionals and amateurs can submit and use materials for video production. Each material receives a unique ID and is licensed for reuse within limits set by its uploader. Registrants can track use through ID declarations submitted by users of their materials. A major distinction from Creative Commons is that registrants may later change usage restrictions. Clarifying previously ambiguous rights offers Niconico's own response to copyright issues shared by video sites. An API enables external integration as well as Niconico Video use. At Niconico Daikaigi 2008, creator SNS pixiv was announced as a participating site.

For details, see Niconi Commons.

### Niconico Channel

Official videos distributed by companies, organizations, and individuals are classified and collected in communities. Videos previously findable only through the Official Video tag are organized into channels, allowing collective viewing by joining the channel community.

For details, see Niconico Channel.

### Niconico Community

A feature for interaction around particular themes. Creators become administrators, managing community-only videos and participant permissions. Internal bulletin boards support interaction with participants and administrators. Joining requires either an invitation from an existing member or an application approved by the administrator. Premium members can create up to 20 communities; ordinary members one. Community level varies with the number of Premium members, determining membership and video-upload limits.

The cyberattack on dedicated file servers in the KADOKAWA Group's data center discovered June 8, 2024 destroyed data and systems needed for recovery. Resumption was deemed impossible and service ended August 5, 2024 [^548].

For details, see Niconico Community.

### Niconico Premium

A paid service for more comfortable use, introduced with RC on June 18, 2007. Payment includes recurring credit-card, PayPal, and carrier billing, or 90-day prepayment using WebMoney and similar methods. Gifts in Niconico Video and Niconico Live also permit Premium tickets to be transferred to ordinary members through 30-, 90-, or 365-day membership gifts. Recipients activate them voluntarily in Ticket Center; multiple entitlements have their durations added together.

Cancellation returns the user to ordinary membership without Premium benefits [^549]. Premium membership numbered 1.71 million at the end of September 2019 [^550].

The following benefits may change with service updates, and some depend on the device used for viewing.[^tn-history]

#### Benefits when watching videos

- View Premium-exclusive videos.
- Select higher-resolution video and higher audio bitrates than ordinary membership's standard quality.
- Hide Nicowari and the marquee area in the HTML5 player.
- Hide pre-roll video advertisements.
- Preview thumbnails by hovering over the seek bar.
- Resume playback from the point where viewing stopped.
- Increase Watch Later capacity from 100 to 500 videos.
- Increase Mylist capacity from 100 to 25,000 videos.
- Create 50 rather than 25 Mylists.
- Follow 50 rather than 20 Mylists.
- Increase blocked-word/user entries from 40 to 200.
- Retain 200 rather than 50 viewing-history entries over the preceding 90 days.
- Use more comment colors.
- Increase maximum playback speed from 1.25× to 2.0×.
- Set separate forward and backward skip durations.
- Hide all advertisements, including banners, except on PC.
- Open unlimited simultaneous viewing tabs rather than three.
- Mirror videos horizontally.
- Display scene thumbnails for comments in each video's comment list.
- Continue audio playback while the app is in the background.
- Create tag-specific Custom Rankings.
- Create five rather than one Custom Rankings.
- Customize five rather than one column on the rankings homepage.
- Specify dates for daily rankings in Ranking Archives.
- Load videos faster than ordinary members.
- Reorder playlists manually.
- Use data-saving viewing.
- Like comments through Nicoru.
- Increase visible Like history from 50 to 1,600 entries.
- Use background playback, accelerated playback, and other features in the iOS/Android Niconico app.

#### Benefits when uploading videos

- Unlimited uploads rather than 50. Ordinary members may also obtain unlimited uploads at levels determined by Premium-member followers.
- Processing priority over ordinary members when uploading during 19:00–02:00 congestion.
- Schedule publication for a specified date and time.
- Upload a chosen video-thumbnail image.
- Upload a chosen series-thumbnail image.
- Change thumbnails of uploaded videos.

#### Benefits when watching live broadcasts

- Priority over ordinary viewers on Niconico Live.
- Catch-up playback of earlier scenes while a program is still live.
- Time-shift viewing until expiration without advance reservations.
- Up to 500 blocked words/users in live comments, versus 40 for ordinary members.
- Hide all advertisements, including banners, except on PC.
- If assigned outside the arena room, comments also appear preferentially in the arena.
- Request Niconama Games and other Broadcast Extras to enliven programs.
- Use every command.
- View Premium-only portions of official live broadcasts.

#### Benefits when broadcasting live

- Extend user broadcasts in 30-minute increments up to 12 hours, regardless of user level; Premium members above a specified level can reach 24 hours.
- Set broadcast quality up to 6 Mbps.
- Download time-shift video data from one's broadcasts.

#### Other benefits

- ~~Create a Blomaga blog.~~ User Blomaga was abolished in 2021.
- No identity-verification procedure when receiving Creator Incentive Program rewards.
- Priority viewing during congestion on Niconico Video Mobile; priority live viewing on Docomo and SoftBank, the latter requiring Wi-Fi.
- Play videos up to 180 minutes on feature-phone Niconico Video Mobile, versus 40 minutes for ordinary members.
- Follow up to 600 communities rather than 100.
- Download and use JASRAC-administered songs registered on Niconi Commons.
- Replace illustrations uploaded to Niconico Seiga.
- Unlimited use of Niconico Shunga's judging feature.
- Register ten rather than five tags in Seiga's illustration monitoring feature.
- View up to 5,000 rather than 100 illustrations through that feature.
- Autoplay manga in Niconico Manga format.
- Submit up to 100 manga works rather than 20.
- Set one's own background music for uploaded manga.
- Register up to 1,000 manga favorites rather than 200.
- Download Niconi Commons works using songs entrusted to JASRAC.
- Publish prepared Niconico Q questions in real time to a limited audience.
- ~~Increase Niconico Jikkyō blocking entries from 40 to 200.~~ Migrated into Niconico Live.
- Create and edit Niconico Pedia articles.
- Remove other users' Niconico Market products, except locked products.
- ~~Obtain stronger signs in StreetPass Marketing within the 3DS version [^551].~~ StreetPass Marketing ended in 2021.
- Early access to new services.

### Niconico Points

A virtual-currency service usable in Niconico Video and related services, launched October 15, 2008 [^552]. Both ordinary and Premium members can use it. Occasional campaigns give points to all Premium members.

Points have expiration periods of several years and expire when their validity ends after purchase.

#### Purchase

Points cost ¥1 each, sold in ¥500, ¥1,000, ¥2,000, ¥3,000, ¥5,000, and ¥10,000 amounts. Since April 1, 2019, credit-card purchases receive a 1% bonus, for example five extra points on 500. Monthly purchase limits apply.

Points expire and cannot pay Premium membership fees [^553]. An auto-charge feature introduced November 14, 2011 automatically purchased points when insufficient [^554], but ended in October 2018 [^555].

Purchase methods are listed below. American Express became available March 23, 2020 [^556].

- Credit cards: VISA, MasterCard, JCB, AMEX. VISA and MasterCard processing is temporarily suspended.
- Convenience-store payment.
- WebMoney.
- BitCash.
- Docomo sp-mode payment.
- d Payment.
- au Easy Payment.
- Rakuten Anshin Payment.
- SoftBank Consolidated Payment and Y!mobile Consolidated Payment.
- Apple Pay.
- Rakuten Edy.
- Rakuten Pay.
- BitCash.
- Rakuten Pay.
- PayPay.
- Paidy next-month payment.

#### Earning free points

Users can obtain specified point awards by registering on sponsor sites and meeting their conditions. These points also expire. Creator Incentive Program scores from Niconi Commons can be exchanged for points. Niconico Market point giveaway campaigns formerly existed too.

#### Uses

Points can be used for:

- Niconi Advertising placements.
- Gift tickets usable on Niconico Live and Niconico Video [^557] [^558].
- Paid Niconico Channel videos, including bundles, but excluding channel monthly membership fees.
- Individual paid Niconico Channel/Blomaga articles [^559].
- Back issues published before the month of joining a paid Blomaga channel [^560].
- Online tickets for paid live broadcasts [^561], except those sold through Dwango Ticket, which does not support points.
- In-game items on Niconico Apps.
- In-game items on Game Atsumaru.
- Ebooks on BOOK☆WALKER, PC and Android versions [^562].

### Niconico Live

A live video-streaming service.

For details, see Niconico Live.

### Niconico Seiga

A portal for still-image services, launched November 14, 2009 as an image board with slideshow playback [^563]. It comprises **Niconico Seiga (Illustrations)**, an illustration-posting site with comments; Niconico Shunga, a somewhat more adult illustration site with comments; **Niconico Seiga (Manga)**, a manga site with comments; and Niconico Books, a website for viewing ebooks purchased with Niconico Points before September 25, 2018.

For details, see Niconico Seiga.

### RPG Atsumaru

A homemade-game submission community for creating, playing, and commenting on games.

For details, see RPG Atsumaru.

### Niconico Apps

A PC browser-game platform.

For details, see Niconico Apps.

### Niconico Q

A new site for conducting surveys and quizzes.

For details, see Niconico Q.

### Niconi Solid

A 3D-model submission and sharing service.

For details, see Niconi Solid.

### Niconico Jikkyō

A service for sharing comments in real time while watching television or listening to radio.

For details, see Niconico Jikkyō.

## Culture

Videos span a wide range of fields without being confined to particular genres. Promotional videos by lesser-known bands have been posted after release; alongside humorous material, uploads include straightforward sports, commercials, and science programs [^617], demonstrating growth as a video-sharing service.

Particular videos sometimes emerge from the enormous collection to receive intense attention. Most trends are temporary, but some remain popular. In early February 2007, the three leading videos—*Shin Gōketsuji Ichizoku: Bonnō Kaihō — Let's Go! Onmyōji PV*, *True My Heart*, and *Aitsu Koso ga Tennis no Ōjisama*—together exceeded 300,000 views and 100,000 comments in a single night in an ongoing “festival” atmosphere. Their popularity also led to exclusive dwango.jp ringtone distribution and advertisements for “Let's Go! Onmyōji” and the Touhou Project derivative song “Marisa Stole the Precious Thing.”

In mid-February 2007, videos of Mizuki Fujisaki (“the god of 2ch”) began distribution. They became popular with the catchphrase “cutting down rotten otaku.” In an extra installment, an excited Fujisaki passionately sang “Okkusenman,” but it was not particularly well received. [The source paragraph contains a grammatical fragment.]

According to Yoshiaki Kataoka, Nobuo Kawakami attributed Niconico's major growth to the synergy between Touhou Project, The Idolmaster, Hatsune Miku, and Tenimyu (*The Prince of Tennis Musical*) [^618].

### Games

Gameplay videos, especially RPGs and action games, have been popular since launch. In contrast to exceptional play, poor play and so-called kusoge (“crap games”), such as *Cheetahmen*, sometimes become popular humorous videos; romance-game gameplay has also gained popularity. More recently, gameplay commentary by ordinary people has become popular [^619].

Promotional-video-style videos of *THE IDOLM@STER* have had a loyal audience among its players since β. Alongside gameplay, many MAD remixes synchronize the game's promotional footage with unrelated music [^620]. The dōjin game series Touhou Project is also highly popular: besides gameplay commentary, there are numerous derivative songs and videos based on its music, and MAD videos using its music and characters [^621].[^tn-mad]

### Music and misheard lyrics

Popular music includes conventional promotional videos and anime songs. Videos making VOCALOID sing various songs are also popular and helped bring Hatsune Miku to public attention [^622]. Some feature original Vocaloid songs written by professional composers, or professional singers performing those originals. MikuMikuDance, a tool for animating Hatsune Miku in 3D, became available; models of non-Vocaloid characters followed, generating 3D video uploads [^623]. Medleys of original or arranged music also appear. *Kumikyoku “Niconico Video”*, linking songs popular on the site, is particularly well known. Many uploaders sing or perform music themselves, and some utaite singers attract substantial followings [^624].[^tn-temita]

Because anyone can easily contribute jokes through comments, *soramimi* (misheard-lyric) jokes have become popular around music from anime and games, including *Aitsu Koso ga Tennis no Ōjisama* and the opening to *Nursery Rhyme*, foreign songs, and overseas anime versions—for example, “Futae no Kiwami, Aaah!” from the English version of *Rurouni Kenshin* [^625].[^tn-soramimi]

A notable feature is that music whose master-recording rights have been licensed through the Japanese Society for Rights of Authors, Composers and Publishers may be used as-is. Eligible songs can be found through niconico Licensed Music Search.[^tn-rights]

### Other content

Videos such as kanji tests, typing exercises, and polls exploit the comment system. Image collections play stills like slideshows, and illustration tutorials appear under categories such as Tried Drawing.

Sophisticated MAD videos and commercial remixes by skilled creators attract great popularity; other videos show everyday scenes, including pets [^626], cooking [^627], and dashboard-camera footage, or feature the uploader in some capacity.

Unlike content less commonly seen on other video sites, companies and individuals show programs or handmade objects they have created, documenting their processes and finished work. They can attach the Niconico Technical Department tag themselves. Content ranges from arts-and-crafts extensions to fully practical “products,” such as mice, PC covers, and video-production software, sustaining a broad and popular category [^628]. Although Niconico Technical Department is merely a convenient classification, volunteers exhibited at O'Reilly Japan's Make:Tokyo Meeting (MTM) on April 20, 2008 and continued participating afterward, extending their activity beyond video subjects [^629].

During *Telebrid* on BS11 on May 30, 2008, host Takeshi Kongōchi made a video for Niconico during the live broadcast. As of June 1, searching Telebrid did not return it.

### April Fools' Day

Since 2008, the following limited versions have appeared on April 1 as [[April Fools Day|April Fools']] projects.

**April 1, 2008 — Niconico Video 2.0 (LOL)**

A Space-Time Reversal feature made comments scroll left to right. Pizza ordering also became available through comments: entering `@ピザ` opened the order screen [^630]. The partnership was with Domino's Pizza, changing to Pizza-La from April 1, 2009 [^631] [^632]; the feature ended May 28, 2024 [^633]. The name referenced the then-current buzzword Web 2.0. See also “(laugh)”.

**April 1, 2009 — Niconico Video (βββ)**

Billed as the fastest version in Niconico history at the time, its High-Speed Player made comments red and three times faster [^634]. The viewing-page HTML used the player parameter `theRevengeOfChar` (“revenge of the characters”). “Char” also matches the English spelling of Char from *Mobile Suit Gundam*, who appears in *Char's Counterattack*. See Char's custom mobile suits for the red color and triple speed.

**April 1, 2010 — Niconico Video Goes into the Black**

The version remained (9), but the site's appearance became monochrome and every comment literally appeared in black [^635]. A “Stop Going into the Black” button displayed “Now in the Red” and turned comments red. This was self-deprecating humor about the business never having made a profit; however, on May 13, 2010 it was reported that it had actually achieved its first profit during the second quarter of fiscal 2010, January 1–March 31 [^98].[^tn-profit]

**April 1, 2011 — Release voluntarily withheld**

The limited April Fools' version was withheld following the March 11 Tōhoku earthquake (Great East Japan Earthquake) and the accident at Tokyo Electric Power Company's Fukushima Daiichi Nuclear Power Station.

**April 1, 2012 — Next-generation player: Touch Implemented** [^636]

The cursor became a real arm and hand; selecting Operate with a Mouse changed it to a hand holding a mouse. A presentation video parodying Apple's product introductions was also released [^637].

Nobuyo Ōyama was introduced as Niconico TV-chan's voice. Deleted-video and time-signal voices were also changed to hers.

**April 1, 2013 — Next-generation player: Zwatch** [^638]

The player became Super Player, surrounded by a yellow aura like a Super Saiyan from *Dragon Ball Z*. Views, comments, Mylist counts, and thumbnails on the right were styled as a scouter. The comment button read “Kamehameha”; recent comments became energy blasts crossing the screen with sound effects. Switching to Super Player God turned the aura red, enlarged the comment blasts, and changed their sound. This was a tie-in with *Dragon Ball Z: Battle of Gods*; Super Player God referenced Super Saiyan God, introduced in that film.

The live player likewise turned all comments into blasts, except on some official broadcasts. Posting a comment containing “Kamehameha” changed its blast color and sound into a Kamehameha. A yellow aura on the broadcast screen could make performers appear to become Super Saiyans.

**April 1, 2014 — nicobot Samba** [^639]

The version remained GINZA. During playback, a Video tab appeared in the usual comment/blocking area; its CLEAN button summoned the **nicobot Samba** robot vacuum from the lower-left corner to clean the video [^6]. After cleaning, it automatically jumped to an operator video with a **nicoionKY** switch that transformed all comments into clean, positive language [^7]. nicobot Samba could be reset below its switch; nicoionKY could be reset at the tab's upper right.

**April 1, 2015 — Voice Comment Input using “the method best able to recognize emotion”; Chūbu Department Store opens**

The official smartphone app implemented voice comment entry [^640]. In practice, users called the Niconico Voice Input Center, which existed for only 24 hours, and orally supplied an operator with the video ID, playback time, and comment; staff entered it manually. Operators taking calls were shown in an official live broadcast [^641]. Over 24 hours, 742 comments were entered by voice [^642].

At Niconico Headquarters in Ikebukuro, the one-day Chūbu Department Store sold merchandise imagining the band TUBE as lying between Tōbu and Seibu [^643].[^tn-chubu]

**April 1, 2016 — Tarako-kun Navigator**

Tarako-kun, a character with thick “cod-roe” lips, appeared on Niconico Pedia articles, showing past Recommended Articles of the Week and operator comments in speech balloons. Quotations from Hiroyuki Nishimura also appeared. It was so unpopular that it ended at 15:00. Brazil later released the Android game Tarako Tataki featuring the character on November 1, 2016; an iOS version followed in June 2017.

**April 1, 2017**

Niconico Pedia's background changed to colorful sticky notes, reverting April 2. The same background returned during Niconico Chōkaigi 2017 on April 29–30.

**April 1, 2018 — Not held**

Following criticism of the niconico (Ku) presentation, no event was held so that efforts could focus on service improvements.

### Summer-only horror versions

**2011 — Yomi (Land of the Dead)**

Appeared by clicking a *fusuma* sliding door hidden somewhere on the homepage [^644].

The design and videos all became horror-themed, in keeping with summer.[^tn-horror]

**2012 — Tokoyami (Eternal Darkness)** [^645]

At the bottom of the homepage, a message read “[username]-chan, why won't you come? Play with me more.” Clicking the Go link opened the version.

Like the previous year's Yomi, it was horror-themed.

**2013 — Cursed Page**

**2014 — Yōkai (Supernatural Creatures)** [^646]

Clicking “Try the new Yōkai version” on the homepage opened it.

Like Yomi and Tokoyami, it used a summery horror theme.

**2015 — Niconi Abandoned School** [^647]

Clicking an image of a blackboard or abandoned school on the homepage opened it.

Like Yomi, Tokoyami, and Yōkai, it was horror-themed.

**2016 — Niconico Inn**

Clicking Reserve under Niconico Inn Reservations on the homepage opened it.

The page initially looked ordinary, but clicking particular places transformed it into a horror page.

**2017 — Niconico-kun's Summer Vacation**

Visiting a user page at night displayed “Strange things happen at night… Did you notice, [username]?” Clicking opened Niconico-kun's Summer Vacation, which became a horror page when scrolled down. Nothing happened when visiting it during the day.

**2018 — Niconico Dating Simulation**

A message appeared on My Page. Clicking opened a page for choosing an ideal partner, who then introduced various videos and live broadcasts.

## Issues

### Copyright

As on other video-sharing sites, most uploaded anime, musical performances, television commercials, and television programs are uploaded without copyright-holder permission. The terms prohibit copyright-infringing uploads.[^tn-rights]

The operators state that they review and delete material when a rights holder—an individual, corporation, or legal representative—reports infringement. Similar treatment applies to portrait rights and other rights. Viewers can report sexually explicit adult content on their own judgment, but copyright complaints are accepted only from rights holders; unrelated individuals or companies acting as proxies cannot secure deletion.

To address unauthorized uploads of complete anime episodes, serial dramas, and films, including Japanese films, corporate users are offered infringement removal/reporting tools [^648]. Since around June 10, 2007, videos removed as infringements have displayed a dedicated notice. Only those whose rights are infringed can request deletion. Some content creators strongly criticized this approach on radio and elsewhere as leaving enforcement to rights holders' own efforts. On October 1, 2007, the company announced stronger rights protection through dialogue, improved protection systems and monitoring, and user education [^649]. On March 11, 2008, shortly after SP1 launched, Niwango told NHK and major networks it would remove existing infringing television-program uploads, strengthen monitoring, and promptly remove reposts [^650] [^651] [^652].

Stricter enforcement sometimes resulted in independent bands' own original-song promotional and concert videos being wrongly deleted as infringement despite being uploaded by their copyright holders [^653]. Since July 5, 2008, removal notices name the corporation or organization requesting deletion [^654]; individual rights holders are identified only as Individual.

At JASRAC's March 25, 2008 symposium “New Distribution Exemplified by Video-Sharing Sites and Copyright,” then Dwango chairman Nobuo Kawakami said: “If the creator doesn't want it, we remove it, whether it's a complete upload or a secondary-use mashup. We're also developing a system that creates something like YouTube's video IDs for automatic detection.” He added that perfect enforcement was impossible, that practical solutions were being sought, and that discussion with individual rights holders should produce deletion rules acceptable to as many people as possible; this acknowledged mistaken deletions [^655].

On April 1, 2008, a blanket music-use agreement with JASRAC made it legally possible to upload videos performing or singing JASRAC-administered songs. Coverage was limited to self-performed music, including singing while playing. Direct use of karaoke tracks or other recordings from CDs or DVDs fell outside the agreement and constituted infringement, so measures such as singing over a self-programmed accompaniment were needed [^656] [^657].

On July 2, 2008, parent Dwango announced that Niwango had consulted with the Motion Picture Producers Association of Japan, Association of Japanese Animations, and Japan Video Software Association following their requests. Niwango proposed promptly deleting videos infringing the member companies' rights, including MAD videos; the associations acknowledged receipt of the written proposal [^658]. However, “hand-drawn MAD” videos that do not use copyrighted source footage have also appeared, making MAD itself an ambiguous boundary.

At Niconico Daikaigi on October 28, 2010, then Kadokawa Group chairman Tsuguhiko Kadokawa stated in a video and at the venue that although not every MAD would be accepted, MAD content expressing love for an anime would be tolerated as far as possible.

As an exception, from July 4, 2011, use of Tarō's master recording “Danjo” on Niconico was authorized at his own wish that many people hear it.

On August 10, 2013, the news site Echo News sent questions about compliance and aggregation sites, alleging that publicly listed Dwango brokered contracts for copyright-infringing aggregation sites through Niconico Video. Neither Dwango nor Niwango answered by the deadline [^659] [^660].

#### Misconduct by uploaders

- In 2009, papers concerning a male company employee were referred to prosecutors on suspicion of violating copyright law by uploading an unreleased song by Japanese rock band DIR EN GREY [^661].
- In May 2011, a man in Kōchi City, Kōchi Prefecture, was arrested for unauthorized uploads of over 40 films [^662].
- In August 2011, an unemployed man was arrested for unauthorized uploads of the latest *Gundam* production [^663].

### Effects of the cyberattack

For details, see Cyberattack on the KADOKAWA Group.

### Other issues

- On November 25, 2020, a YouTuber's video investigating the Sasayama discriminatory graffiti incidents on location and exploring the psychological mechanisms of discrimination appeared on YouTube, Niconico Video, and Livedoor Blog (LINE). The neighborhood association considered it discriminatory and consulted Tamba-Sasayama City, Hyōgo Prefecture. The city judged it to infringe residents' privacy and reputation, and requested removal. Site administrators refused, considering it protected freedom of expression. Between October and December 2020, the city and neighborhood association chairman jointly sought provisional orders against the three companies. YouTube and Livedoor Blog subsequently saw voluntary removal by their administrators. Dwango instead stated that it had checked the content but would not remove it voluntarily and would contest the matter. In February 2021, Tamba-Sasayama and the association sought a provisional injunction against Dwango on the grounds that the video encouraged discrimination; in May, the Kashiwabara branch of Kobe District Court ordered removal. Dwango removed the video following the decision recognizing, among other things, invasion of the association chairman's privacy. The city ultimately withdrew as an applicant because it was not a direct party and to obtain a prompt decision. Mayor Takaaki Sakai explained that although the city had actively assisted the applicant, eliminating buraku discrimination was its responsibility and a longstanding endeavor; he maintained that this did not infringe freedom of expression. This was Japan's first provisional order requiring removal of a video addressing buraku discrimination [^664] [^665] [^666].[^tn-buraku]

## Companies associated with Niconico

### KADOKAWA Group

**KADOKAWA**

Dwango's parent company, established in October 2014 as the merger company of KADOKAWA (now KADOKAWA KEY-PROCESS) and Dwango. Supplies anime, manga, and other content.

**[[Dwango]]**

Niconico's operating company.

**Custom Cast Co., Ltd.**

A joint venture between Dwango and S-Court operating the 3D-avatar app Custom Cast, integrated with Virtual Live Streaming in the Niconico Live smartphone app.

**Virtual Cast Co., Ltd.**

Dwango subsidiary operating the VR live-communication service Virtual Cast.

### Partner companies

**[[Mirai Kensaku Brazil(Future Search Brazil)|Brazil]]**

Former operator of niconico's Niconico Pedia and former shareholder of [[Niwango]]. Its Moritapo payment system supports Premium fees.

**Avex Entertainment**

Operates an official page distributing promotional and original videos featuring its artists. Although chiefly promotional videos, some artists, such as Haruko Momoi, produce original humorous videos seemingly aimed at Niconico users. On December 20, 2010, the company's president announced on Hisashi Yamada's Nicoradio that Avex-released songs could be used on Niconico [^667] [^668]. Dwango was formerly an equity-method affiliate of Avex Group Holdings.

**Yoshimoto Kōgyō Group**

Opened the official Yoshiyoshi Video page [^44] [^45]. Dwango and KADOKAWA hold Yoshimoto Kōgyō shares.

**Aniplex**

Opened a Sekirei channel in July 2010, distributing television anime within Niconico Video.

**China Central Television**

Announced a Dwango partnership in August 2016 [^168] and opened an official CCTV channel [^669]. Distributes speeches by the President of China (Chinese Communist Party General Secretary), People's Liberation Army press conferences, and similar material [^670]. Former Dwango chairman Kawakami is known for repeatedly defending the People's Republic of China's Internet-management policies [^671] [^672].

**Nintendo**

Nintendo Network and Nintendo Accounts can be used for Niconico registration and login. Videos can be watched through niconico for Nintendo Switch and Niconico for Nintendo 3DS.

**Sony Interactive Entertainment**

PlayStation Network permits registration and login. PlayStation 4 supports live-game streaming and viewing. The former PlayStation Vita Niconico app allowed video/live viewing; that service has ended.

**LY Corporation (formerly Yahoo Japan)**

Yahoo! JAPAN IDs permit registration and login [^673].

**Google**

Google Accounts permit registration and login. Google advertisements are used for niconico video and banner ads.

**Apple**

Apple IDs permit registration and login, provided the ID is associated with an iPhone, iPod touch, iPad, or Macintosh.

**Twitter**

Twitter accounts permit registration and login.

**Facebook**

Facebook accounts permit registration and login.

**LINE**

LINE accounts permit registration and login.

**Amazon.com**

Niconico Video's in-stream advertising connects to Transparent Ad Marketplace, the header-bidding solution of Amazon Publisher Services [^674].

**i-mobile**

The i-mobile ad network supplies niconico banner advertisements.

**Criteo**

Criteo supplies banner advertisements.

- Max Matsuura: when Avex invested in Dwango, instructed Kawakami to build a service like mixi; the resulting service was Niconico Video.

> [!info] Cultural context and translator's notes
> **Reading this clipping.** This is a translation of the saved Japanese Wikipedia article, including its chronology, version tables, service descriptions, navigation templates, and references. Statements such as “now,” planned features, availability, membership benefits, and payment restrictions retain the source's perspective. The article combines material from different years and contains inconsistencies: for example, the history and version table disagree on some dates, a feature row gives March 2006 for Mylist rankings, and some discontinued functions are described in the present tense. These have been preserved rather than silently harmonized. Translator footnotes use `tn-` identifiers; numbered footnotes belong to the original article.
>
> **Watching together at different times.** The important cultural distinction is that a Niconico comment is attached to a moment *inside a video*. A viewer arriving years later can encounter reactions written earlier at that same dramatic moment. The article's “pseudo-synchrony” explains how repeated jokes, sung responses, and coordinated-looking barrages can feel communal without a simultaneous audience. *Danmaku* literally evokes a barrage, while *shokunin* calls skilled comment artists “craftspeople.” These terms describe forms of participation, not formal employment or site moderation.
>
> **Participatory genres.** *Utattemita*, *odottemita*, and *kaitemita* use the modest “tried doing…” construction for singing, dancing, and drawing. They are established upload genres, not necessarily beginner attempts. *Utaite* labels performers in the singing scene; *Vocalo-P* denotes Vocaloid producers. MAD is retained for fan-edited remix videos. The source distinguishes source-footage remixes from hand-drawn MAD, so the label alone does not identify how a work was made or determine its rights status.
>
> **Language as collective play.** *Soramimi* jokes turn a sound into another imagined phrase, often making foreign lyrics sound Japanese. Tags can likewise act as punchlines or conversations, beyond their search function. “A waste of talent” is often humorous appreciation for elaborate work devoted to an absurd purpose; *owacon*, shortened from “finished content,” dismisses something as past its prime. *Matsuri* (“festival”) describes a burst of collective online excitement here. Its meaning depends on context and does not by itself imply an organized offline event.
>
> **Service-name wordplay.** *Niconico* evokes smiling; *dōga* means moving images/video, while *seiga* means still images. The article switches between the video service and the umbrella brand, and its 2020 renaming changes the written branding from Latin letters to Japanese katakana. Nicoru is the platform's coined liking verb and should be distinguished from the separately listed Like feature. Version names include software terminology, Tokyo place names, and puns; explanatory translations are not claims to be official English branding.
>
> **Seasonal and parody references.** The summer horror pages draw on the source's stated association between summer and horror; *Yomi*, *tokoyami*, and *yōkai* are rendered as Land of the Dead, Eternal Darkness, and Supernatural Creatures to make their mood legible. The April Fools' examples explicitly reference Gundam, Dragon Ball, Apple presentations, and Japanese financial vocabulary. In the 2015 department-store joke, Tōbu, Seibu, and the invented Chūbu play on east, west, and middle; the band name TUBE supplies the sound pun.
>
> **Rights and discrimination.** Copyright passages report what this historical source says, including allegations and attributed statements; they are not a current licensing guide. The distinction between a musical composition and a particular sound recording matters to the JASRAC discussion. In the legal dispute about a local video, *buraku discrimination* has a specific meaning: discrimination associated with historically stigmatized communities and inherited social status. Translating it simply as “village discrimination” would conceal that context. Background on the historical status system is provided by the [Buraku Liberation and Human Rights Research Institute](https://blhrri.org/old/blhrri_e/Q_and_A/A003.htm).

[^tn-naming]: *Niconico* (ニコニコ) evokes smiling; *dōga* (動画) means moving images or video. “Niconico Video” translates the service name; the umbrella brand is also called Niconico. The source's 2020 change is specifically from Latin-letter `niconico` to Japanese `ニコニコ`. In the founding anecdote, *black* describes shady business practices rather than a literal color or racial category; the source links it to the Japanese “black company” concept.
[^tn-danmaku]: 弾幕 means a barrage or curtain of projectiles. Here it describes dense comments covering the picture, not gunfire. The source also uses the term for Chinese imitator platforms' overlaid-comment systems.
[^tn-matsuri]: 祭り (*matsuri*, festival) is online slang for a concentrated burst of participation, jokes, comments, or excitement. The anniversary “festival” is an online reaction in this passage.
[^tn-mobile]: *Chaku-uta* were downloadable song recordings used as mobile ringtones; *chaku-uta Full* designates the full-length counterpart. The source distinguishes these services from ordinary video distribution.
[^tn-temita]: ～してみた means “tried doing…”. On Niconico, 歌ってみた (*utattemita*), 踊ってみた (*odottemita*), and 描いてみた (*kaitemita*) label singing, dancing, and drawing genres. The phrasing need not imply low skill. 歌い手 (*utaite*) is retained when referring to the singing scene; 職人 (*shokunin*) figuratively honors skilled comment makers as craftspeople.
[^tn-counts]: These are account/membership statistics, not a count of distinct people. The source explicitly permits multiple accounts and records a change in counting method from issued IDs to valid memberships.
[^tn-history]: Dates, planned features, and apparent inconsistencies are preserved from the clipping. The chronology and tables do not always agree; benefits and restrictions described in the present tense can belong to different historical service states. No factual claims have been silently updated to the translation date.
[^tn-hours]: Japanese schedules sometimes continue the same day's clock past midnight: 26:00 means 02:00 the following calendar day.
[^tn-nicoru]: ニコる (*Nicoru*) turns the platform name into a verb for giving appreciation. It is distinct from the separate いいね! (“Like”) feature described elsewhere in the article.
[^tn-tags]: “A waste of talent” (才能の無駄遣い) can be admiringly ironic. “Kōmei's trap” (孔明の罠) casts an unexpected trick as the work of the strategist Kōmei. Both are examples of tags interpreting or joking about a video, beyond classifying it.
[^tn-mad]: MAD is retained as the Japanese fan-remix genre label, rather than translated as the English adjective “mad.” The source includes edited footage, music substitutions, and hand-drawn derivatives under related usages.
[^tn-soramimi]: 空耳 (*soramimi*) is rendered “misheard lyrics” in this music context. The joke depends on phonetic resemblance: listeners supply a new phrase rather than a literal translation of the original words.
[^tn-rights]: This translation preserves the source's copyright claims and its own distinctions between composition licensing, recordings, rights-holder complaints, and tolerated fan works. It does not establish current permission to reuse a particular work; the earlier statement about master-recording licensing and the later JASRAC discussion should not be conflated.
[^tn-profit]: 黒字 (*kuroji*, black figures) means a profit; 赤字 (*akaji*, red figures) means a deficit. Turning comment text black or red makes those financial expressions literal.
[^tn-chubu]: The invented 中武 (*Chūbu*) places “middle” between 東武 (*Tōbu*, east) and 西武 (*Seibu*, west), while sounding like TUBE. These are the elements of the department-store parody described in the source.
[^tn-horror]: The source explicitly presents horror as a summer theme. *Fusuma* are sliding partition doors; Yomi is a land of the dead, *tokoyami* means enduring darkness, and *yōkai* refers broadly to supernatural creatures or phenomena.
[^tn-buraku]: Here 部落 refers to communities targeted by a historically rooted status-based discrimination, not merely an ordinary hamlet. The [Buraku Liberation and Human Rights Research Institute's historical explanation](https://blhrri.org/old/blhrri_e/Q_and_A/A003.htm) links the formation of the discriminatory status structure to the Toyotomi/early Edo period. This contextual note does not add a judgment about the individual case.
[^tn-owacon]: オワコン (*owacon*) abbreviates 終わったコンテンツ, “finished content,” implying something has lost relevance. Kurita's “long-established business” remark deliberately turns that dismissal into a joke about longevity.
